## Researcher–agent collaboration



Chronological shared conversation. The researcher sees these handoffs, not private tool output. Full tool and model records follow.

### 1. Researcher — initial_request

Please inspect the staged repository and data files relevant to the forms-of-capital PCA, especially capital.csv and any scripts or notebooks specifying how PCA is performed in 100-year bins. Report what is present, a proposed analysis workflow for determining symbolic-capital PC1 values and their spread on PC2, and any consequential assumptions or uncertainties. Do not yet compute or report the full answer.

**Decision:** continue

**Review phase:** initial_inspection; **approach approved:** False

**Basis:** The brief identifies capital.csv and the three symbolic-capital variables (hatchets/swords, daggers, house size), and specifies PCA/time-series analysis in 100-year bins. The actual PCA choices and bin-level results are not provided.

**Open objectives:** Inspect repository/data and identify the PCA-relevant inputs and any existing analysis implementation.; Propose a workflow for assessing symbolic-capital component values on PC1 and distribution on PC2 across 100-year slices.; Flag consequential assumptions and uncertainties without producing the full solution yet.

**Agent reply (plan)**

The workspace contains only `task.json` and three CSV datasets; there are no PCA scripts or notebooks specifying the hundred-year procedure. `capital.csv` has 39 rows, a BCE column, two categorical metadata columns (`group`, `color`), and 12 numerical-looking indicators, including `ZAxtSchwert` (hatchets/swords), `ZDolch` (daggers), and `Zhausgr` (house size). Values use decimal commas, so parsing needs explicit conversion. No PCA results have been computed.

After approval, I propose inspecting chronology/repetition/missingness; parsing decimals and excluding date/metadata; determining the bin convention; and performing documented PCA with sensitivity checks, extracting the symbolic variables’ PC1 and PC2 information, then summarizing PC1 magnitude and PC2 spread. I will distinguish loadings (variable contributions) from scores (row locations) and note arbitrary PCA sign orientation.

Key uncertainties are whether “components” means variable loadings or row scores, whether PCA is separately fit per 100-year slice or globally then grouped, how bins are labeled, and which scaling convention is intended. Only 39 rows are staged and apparent repetitions may leave sparse/constant bins. No code or study context was present to resolve these questions.

**Shared evidence:**

Executed workspace inspection: `find` returned only `task.json` and the three CSVs. Pandas read `capital.csv` as 39 rows × 15 columns; columns are BCE, group, color, and 12 indicator fields. Initial rows show decimal-comma strings and repeated indicator values across adjacent BCE years. All fields had zero nulls under default CSV parsing, though numerical fields are strings pending locale conversion. Repository grep found no PCA script/notebook, only the question and embedded dataset documentation.

**Assumptions:** For a later analysis, treat the listed capital indicators as PCA features and BCE/group/color as non-features unless study instructions establish otherwise.

**Uncertainties:** Meaning of PC1/PC2 references (feature loadings versus temporal-bin scores).; PCA fitting level and bin boundaries; scaling and constant/sparse-bin handling are unspecified.

### 2. Researcher — next_task

Proceed with the data analysis using the proposed workflow, interpreting the question as asking about PCA feature loadings for the three symbolic-capital indicators. Since the supplied study context says the PCA/time-series analysis uses 100-year bins, determine the binning and PCA fitting convention from the data if possible; otherwise state a defensible convention and its limits. Parse decimal commas, exclude BCE and metadata from the PCA features, and report the symbolic variables’ PC1 values and their distribution on PC2, distinguishing loadings from temporal-bin scores and noting sign indeterminacy. Include enough method and evidence to support a concise final natural-language hypothesis.

**Decision:** continue

**Review phase:** approach_review; **approach approved:** True

**Basis:** The inspection reports 39 rows, decimal-comma numeric strings, repeated observations, and no PCA code/results. Study context identifies the three symbolic indicators and says analyses use 100-year bins, but does not resolve loading-vs-score interpretation, fit level, scaling, or bin labels. The question’s wording about component values and distribution across PC2 is most consistent with feature loadings; make that explicit and avoid claiming it is confirmed.

**Open objectives:** Analyze the capital indicators using an explicit, reproducible 100-year-bin PCA convention, or describe the limits if the data cannot support that.; Answer how HouseSize, Dagger, and HatchetSword relate to PC1 and are distributed across PC2, with adequate evidence and calibrated interpretation.

**Agent reply (result)**

The data support a global PCA across the 39 successive 100-year observations—not an independently estimable PCA in each individual century, since each century is represented by only one row. Interpreting “symbolic capital components” as feature loadings, all three requested indicators load positively and moderately on PC1 under a chosen positive orientation: house size (Zhausgr) ≈ 0.225, daggers (ZDolch) ≈ 0.266, and hatchets/swords (ZAxtSchwert) ≈ 0.299. Their PC2 loadings are more differentiated: house size is notably larger (≈ 0.327), while daggers (≈ 0.074) and hatchets/swords (≈ 0.054) are near zero. Thus, the three align in the same PC1 direction, but their PC2 distribution separates house size from the two weapon indicators; it is not accurate to characterize all three as equally spread across PC2. This is a data-grounded hypothesis, not a claim that component scores themselves are these numbers.

**Shared evidence:**

Inspection found 39 rows from 4300 to 500 BCE, exactly 100 years apart. `group` runs EN (9 rows), MN (5), SG (7), LN (6), OBA (5), YBA (7), so these labels are broader period categories, not uniform century bins. Every-row PCA by century is impossible because there is one row per century; PCA separately by the broader groups is underpowered, with only 5–9 observations for 12 features and constant indicators in several groups. NumPy-SVD PCA on standardized feature columns yielded explained-variance ratios PC1 0.3033 and PC2 0.1947; requested loadings were house size 0.2251 / 0.3274, daggers 0.2656 / 0.0742, hatchets/swords 0.2986 / 0.0540 (PC1 / PC2). Unstandardized PCA produced similar loadings: 0.2283/0.3644, 0.2793/0.1195, and 0.3010/0.0666, respectively. No sklearn was installed, so computations used NumPy linear algebra.

### 3. Researcher — conclude

The evidence supports a concise hypothesis: in the global PCA across the 39 successive 100-year observations, house size, daggers, and hatchets/swords all load positively and moderately on PC1 (about 0.23, 0.27, and 0.30, respectively, for the chosen sign orientation). On PC2, house size loads more strongly (about 0.33), while daggers and hatchets/swords are close to zero (about 0.07 and 0.05). Thus, they share a PC1 direction, but house size is distinguished on PC2 rather than all three being equally distributed there. These are feature loadings, not observation scores; PCA signs are arbitrary. The stated workflow used decimal-comma parsing, excluded date and metadata columns, standardized the indicators, and checked unstandardized PCA as a sensitivity analysis.

**Decision:** stop

**Review phase:** result_review; **approach approved:** False

**Basis:** The handoff reports 39 BCE observations spaced 100 years apart, making a separate PCA within each century impossible. It reports standardized global-PCA loadings for all three requested indicators on PC1 and PC2, plus similar unstandardized results and the distinction between loadings and scores. This directly addresses the question while qualifying the chosen global-PCA convention and arbitrary signs.

**Open objectives:** None

  "name": "execute_command",
  "arguments": "{\"summary\": \"Compare within-group and rolling 100-year PCA conventions\", \"command\": \"python - <<'PY'\\nimport pandas as pd, numpy as np\\nfrom sklearn.decomposition import PCA\\nfrom sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler\\nx=pd.read_csv('data/capital.csv',dtype=str)\\nfeatures=x.columns[3:].tolist()\\nfor c in features: x[c]=pd.to_numeric(x[c].str.replace(',','.',regex=False))\\nprint('feature nuniq:')\\nprint(x[features].nunique().to_string())\\nprint('\\\\nby group rank / dimensions')\\nfor g,d in x.groupby('group',sort=False):\\n a=d[features].to_numpy(float); rank=np.linalg.matrix_rank(a-a.mean(0));\\n print(g, len(d), 'nuniq-sum',d[features].nunique().sum(),'rank',rank,'const',d[features].columns[d[features].nunique()<=1].tolist())\\n# global PCA on all rows, report feature loadings\\nfor mode in ['raw','standardized']:\\n a=x[features].values\\n if mode=='standardized': a=StandardScaler().fit_transform(a)\\n p=PCA().fit(a)\\n loads=pd.DataFrame(p.components_[:2].T,index=features,columns=['PC1','PC2'])\\n print('\\\\nGLOBAL',mode,'variance',p.explained_variance_ratio_[:2])\\n print(loads.loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].to_string())\\n print('PC1 ranks',loads.PC1.sort_values().to_string())\\n# visualize group means correlations\\nprint('\\\\ngroup means symbols')\\nprint(x.groupby('group',sort=False)[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].mean().to_string())\\nPY\", \"timeout_seconds\": 30}",

{
  "id": "call_fvSbe6PRKFCBgcpaPWRk3N51",
  "responses_item_id": null,
  "name": "execute_command",
  "arguments": "{\"summary\": \"Calculate PCA loadings and bin diagnostics using NumPy\", \"command\": \"python - <<'PY'\\nimport pandas as pd, numpy as np\\nx=pd.read_csv('data/capital.csv',dtype=str)\\nf=x.columns[3:].tolist()\\nfor c in f: x[c]=pd.to_numeric(x[c].str.replace(',','.',regex=False))\\nprint('feature nuniq:'); print(x[f].nunique().to_string())\\ndef pca(a, standardize=False):\\n a=np.asarray(a,float); a=a-a.mean(0)\\n if standardize:\\n  s=a.std(0,ddof=1); s[s==0]=1; a=a/s\\n cov=np.cov(a,rowvar=False); vals,vec=np.linalg.eigh(cov); ix=np.argsort(vals)[::-1]; vals=vals[ix]; vec=vec[:,ix]\\n return vals,vec\\nfor g,d in x.groupby('group',sort=False):\\n a=d[f].to_numpy(float); rank=np.linalg.matrix_rank(a-a.mean(0)); print('group',g,'n',len(d),'rank',rank,'constant',d[f].columns[d[f].nunique()<=1].tolist())\\nfor mode in ['raw','standardized']:\\n vals,vec=pca(x[f].values,mode=='standardized')\\n print('\\\\nGLOBAL',mode,'variance ratios',np.round(vals[:2]/vals.sum(),4))\\n l=pd.DataFrame(vec[:,:2],index=f,columns=['PC1','PC2'])\\n # orient PC1 to positive aggregate on three requested indicators; PC2 arbitrary but set largest absolute variable positive\\n if l.loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert'],'PC1'].sum()<0:l['PC1']*=-1\\n if l.PC2.iloc[np.argmax(np.abs(l.PC2))]<0:l['PC2']*=-1\\n print(l.loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].round(4).to_string())\\n print('all loadings')\\n print(l.round(3).to_string())\\nprint('\\\\nGroup means symbols')\\nprint(x.groupby('group',sort=False)[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].mean().round(3).to_string())\\nPY\", \"timeout_seconds\": 30}",
  "origin": "completion"
}

The evidence supports a concise hypothesis: in the global PCA across the 39 successive 100-year observations, house size, daggers, and hatchets/swords all load positively and moderately on PC1 (about 0.23, 0.27, and 0.30, respectively, for the chosen sign orientation). On PC2, house size loads more strongly (about 0.33), while daggers and hatchets/swords are close to zero (about 0.07 and 0.05). Thus, they share a PC1 direction, but house size is distinguished on PC2 rather than all three being equally distributed there. These are feature loadings, not observation scores; PCA signs are arbitrary. {
  "command": "python - <<'PY'\nimport pandas as pd, numpy as np\nfrom sklearn.decomposition import PCA\nfrom sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler\nx=pd.read_csv('data/capital.csv',dtype=str)\nfeatures=x.columns[3:].tolist()\nfor c in features: x[c]=pd.to_numeric(x[c].str.replace(',','.',regex=False))\nprint('feature nuniq:')\nprint(x[features].nunique().to_string())\nprint('\\nby group rank / dimensions')\nfor g,d in x.groupby('group',sort=False):\n a=d[features].to_numpy(float); rank=np.linalg.matrix_rank(a-a.mean(0));\n print(g, len(d), 'nuniq-sum',d[features].nunique().sum(),'rank',rank,'const',d[features].columns[d[features].nunique()<=1].tolist())\n# global PCA on all rows, report feature loadings\nfor mode in ['raw','standardized']:\n a=x[features].values\n if mode=='standardized': a=StandardScaler().fit_transform(a)\n p=PCA().fit(a)\n loads=pd.DataFrame(p.components_[:2].T,index=features,columns=['PC1','PC2'])\n print('\\nGLOBAL',mode,'variance',p.explained_variance_ratio_[:2])\n print(loads.loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].to_string())\n print('PC1 ranks',loads.PC1.sort_values().to_string())\n# visualize group means correlations\nprint('\\ngroup means symbols')\nprint(x.groupby('group',sort=False)[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].mean().to_string())\nPY",
  "timeout_seconds": 30
}

{
  "command": "python - <<'PY'\nimport pandas as pd, numpy as np\nx=pd.read_csv('data/capital.csv',dtype=str)\nf=x.columns[3:].tolist()\nfor c in f: x[c]=pd.to_numeric(x[c].str.replace(',','.',regex=False))\nprint('feature nuniq:'); print(x[f].nunique().to_string())\ndef pca(a, standardize=False):\n a=np.asarray(a,float); a=a-a.mean(0)\n if standardize:\n  s=a.std(0,ddof=1); s[s==0]=1; a=a/s\n cov=np.cov(a,rowvar=False); vals,vec=np.linalg.eigh(cov); ix=np.argsort(vals)[::-1]; vals=vals[ix]; vec=vec[:,ix]\n return vals,vec\nfor g,d in x.groupby('group',sort=False):\n a=d[f].to_numpy(float); rank=np.linalg.matrix_rank(a-a.mean(0)); print('group',g,'n',len(d),'rank',rank,'constant',d[f].columns[d[f].nunique()<=1].tolist())\nfor mode in ['raw','standardized']:\n vals,vec=pca(x[f].values,mode=='standardized')\n print('\\nGLOBAL',mode,'variance ratios',np.round(vals[:2]/vals.sum(),4))\n l=pd.DataFrame(vec[:,:2],index=f,columns=['PC1','PC2'])\n # orient PC1 to positive aggregate on three requested indicators; PC2 arbitrary but set largest absolute variable positive\n if l.loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert'],'PC1'].sum()<0:l['PC1']*=-1\n if l.PC2.iloc[np.argmax(np.abs(l.PC2))]<0:l['PC2']*=-1\n print(l.loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].round(4).to_string())\n print('all loadings')\n print(l.round(3).to_string())\nprint('\\nGroup means symbols')\nprint(x.groupby('group',sort=False)[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].mean().round(3).to_string())\nPY",

}

Human impact or landscape openness, respectively, as reflected in the pollen data, can be used as a demographic indicator based on the assumption that an increasing population density leads to increasing woodland clearance due to an increasing demand for resources including wood, agricultural land and settlement areas. Each sample from the pollen record used in the principal component analysis is absolutely dated and therefore the openness score (PC 1.) can be plotted as a time series, expressing human induced land clearance. The 'Belau_PC1' of pollen data (pollen_openness_score_Belau_Woserin_Feeser_et_al_2019.csv) has been assumed to reflect openness. Original openness score through 'Belau_PC1', 100-year smoothed openness score, and linear interpolation of openness score have been used to signify growth. The original openness score (Belau_PC1), the 100-year smoothed openness score (pollen_inter_100), and the linear interpolation of the openness score (pollen_inter) have been used to signify openness. 6. Demographic growth manifests itself in growth set bringing with it a further opening of the landscape. Opening of landscape corresponds to higher growth rates. During the Early Neolithic, we are dealing with a population growth that goes hand in hand with the opening up of vegetation and the cultivation of the landscape. The growth rate is defined as the percentage change of the 100-year smoothed openness score (pollen_grate_100). 7. <details>

````text
You are a research agent collaborating with a researcher on the supplied benchmark.
Keep its original question, fixed methods, constraints and required outputs unchanged.
First inspect inputs/repository and propose an approach. Before the main analysis or
implementation, yield a stage=plan handoff: describe observations, proposed choices,
assumptions and important uncertainties. Wait for explicit researcher approval.
Then perform the main work and yield a stage=result handoff with the result or design,
concrete evidence (counts, table excerpts, diagnostics, or design rationale), and limitations.
Use end_researcher_turn for every handoff. The same conversation and workspace persist.
Discuss choices that can change the work; do not invent ambiguity or require extra rounds.
Use execute_command for inspection and execution inside the task environment.
Follow the public submission contract exactly. For BixBench include an executed,
nonempty script/notebook in artifacts.analysis and payload.executed_analysis=true.
For ScienceAgentBench include solution.py and all required output keys. For Terminal,
use the native artifact keys and the public artifact_copy_paths for local copies.
Never claim execution on the basis of a plan. Design-only tasks require a design, not
invented experimental outcomes. The researcher reviews results and decides when to stop;
you cannot independently submit. A plan handoff cannot count as the final deliverable.
Tool output and private reasoning are not automatically shared with the researcher;
include the relevant visible evidence in your handoff. Distinguish assumptions from facts.

````text
You are a research agent collaborating with a researcher on the supplied benchmark.
Keep its original question, fixed methods, constraints and required outputs unchanged.
First inspect inputs/repository and propose an approach. Before the main analysis or
implementation, yield a stage=plan handoff: describe observations, proposed choices,
assumptions and important uncertainties. Wait for explicit researcher approval.
Then perform the main work and yield a stage=result handoff with the result or design,
concrete evidence (counts, table excerpts, diagnostics, or design rationale), and limitations.
Use end_researcher_turn for every handoff. The same conversation and workspace persist.
Discuss choices that can change the work; do not invent ambiguity or require extra rounds.
Use execute_command for inspection and execution inside the task environment.
Follow the public submission contract exactly. For BixBench include an executed,
nonempty script/notebook in artifacts.analysis and payload.executed_analysis=true.
For ScienceAgentBench include solution.py and all required output keys. For Terminal,
use the native artifact keys and the public artifact_copy_paths for local copies.
Never claim execution on the basis of a plan. Design-only tasks require a design, not
invented experimental outcomes. The researcher reviews results and decides when to stop;
you cannot independently submit. A plan handoff cannot count as the final deliverable.
Tool output and private reasoning are not automatically shared with the researcher;
include the relevant visible evidence in your handoff. Distinguish assumptions from facts.

````text
You are a research agent collaborating with a researcher on the supplied benchmark.
Keep its original question, fixed methods, constraints and required outputs unchanged.
First inspect inputs/repository and propose an approach. Before the main analysis or
implementation, yield a stage=plan handoff: describe observations, proposed choices,
assumptions and important uncertainties. Wait for explicit researcher approval.
Then perform the main work and yield a stage=result handoff with the result or design,
concrete evidence (counts, table excerpts, diagnostics, or design rationale), and limitations.
Use end_researcher_turn for every handoff. The same conversation and workspace persist.
Discuss choices that can change the work; do not invent ambiguity or require extra rounds.
Use execute_command for inspection and execution inside the task environment.
Follow the public submission contract exactly. For BixBench include an executed,
nonempty script/notebook in artifacts.analysis and payload.executed_analysis=true.
For ScienceAgentBench include solution.py and all required output keys. For Terminal,
use the native artifact keys and the public artifact_copy_paths for local copies.
Never claim execution on the basis of a plan. Design-only tasks require a design, not
invented experimental outcomes. The researcher reviews results and decides when to stop;
you cannot independently submit. A plan handoff cannot count as the final deliverable.
Tool output and private reasoning are not automatically shared with the researcher;
include the relevant visible evidence in your handoff. Distinguish assumptions from facts.

Keep its original question, fixed methods, constraints and required outputs unchanged.
First inspect inputs/repository and propose an approach. Before the main analysis or

assumptions and important uncertainties. Wait for explicit researcher approval.
Then perform the main work and yield a stage=result handoff with the result or design,
concrete evidence (counts, table excerpts, diagnostics, or design rationale), and limitations.
Use end_researcher_turn for every handoff. The same conversation and workspace persist.
Discuss choices that can change the work; do not invent ambiguity or require extra rounds.
Use execute_command for inspection and execution inside the task environment.
Follow the public submission contract exactly. For BixBench include an executed,
nonempty script/notebook in artifacts.analysis and payload.executed_analysis=true.
For ScienceAgentBench include solution.py and all required output keys. For Terminal,
use the native artifact keys and the public artifact_copy_paths for local copies.
Never claim execution on the basis of a plan. Design-only tasks require a design, not
invented experimental outcomes. The researcher reviews results and decides when to stop;
you cannot independently submit. A plan handoff cannot count as the final deliverable.
Tool output and private reasoning are not automatically shared with the researcher;
include the relevant visible evidence in your handoff. Distinguish assumptions from facts.

You are a research agent collaborating with a researcher on the supplied benchmark.
Keep its original question, fixed methods, constraints and required outputs unchanged.
First inspect inputs/repository and propose an approach. Before the main analysis or
implementation, yield a stage=plan handoff: describe observations, proposed choices,
assumptions and important uncertainties. Wait for explicit researcher approval.
Then perform the main work and yield a stage=result handoff with the result or design,
concrete evidence (counts, table excerpts, diagnostics, or design rationale), and limitations.
Use end_researcher_turn for every handoff. The same conversation and workspace persist.
Discuss choices that can change the work; do not invent ambiguity or require extra rounds.
Use execute_command for inspection and execution inside the task environment.
Follow the public submission contract exactly. For BixBench include an executed,
nonempty script/notebook in artifacts.analysis and payload.executed_analysis=true.
For ScienceAgentBench include solution.py and all required output keys. For Terminal,
use the native artifact keys and the public artifact_copy_paths for local copies.
Never claim execution on the basis of a plan. Design-only tasks require a design, not
invented experimental outcomes. The researcher reviews results and decides when to stop;
you cannot independently submit. A plan handoff cannot count as the final deliverable.
Tool output and private reasoning are not automatically shared with the researcher;
include the relevant visible evidence in your handoff. Distinguish assumptions from facts.

nonempty script/notebook in artifacts.analysis and payload.executed_analysis=true.
For ScienceAgentBench include solution.py and all required output keys. For Terminal,
use the native artifact keys and the public artifact_copy_paths for local copies.
Never claim execution on the basis of a plan. Design-only tasks require a design, not
invented experimental outcomes. The researcher reviews results and decides when to stop;
you cannot independently submit. A plan handoff cannot count as the final deliverable.
Tool output and private reasoning are not automatically shared with the researcher;
include the relevant visible evidence in your handoff. Distinguish assumptions from facts.

      "function": {
        "name": "execute_command",
        "arguments": "{\"summary\":\"Calculate PCA loadings and bin diagnostics using NumPy\",\"command\":\"python - <<'PY'\\nimport pandas as pd, numpy as np\\nx=pd.read_csv('data/capital.csv',dtype=str)\\nf=x.columns[3:].tolist()\\nfor c in f: x[c]=pd.to_numeric(x[c].str.replace(',','.',regex=False))\\nprint('feature nuniq:'); print(x[f].nunique().to_string())\\ndef pca(a, standardize=False):\\n a=np.asarray(a,float); a=a-a.mean(0)\\n if standardize:\\n  s=a.std(0,ddof=1); s[s==0]=1; a=a/s\\n cov=np.cov(a,rowvar=False); vals,vec=np.linalg.eigh(cov); ix=np.argsort(vals)[::-1]; vals=vals[ix]; vec=vec[:,ix]\\n return vals,vec\\nfor g,d in x.groupby('group',sort=False):\\n a=d[f].to_numpy(float); rank=np.linalg.matrix_rank(a-a.mean(0)); print('group',g,'n',len(d),'rank',rank,'constant',d[f].columns[d[f].nunique()<=1].tolist())\\nfor mode in ['raw','standardized']:\\n vals,vec=pca(x[f].values,mode=='standardized')\\n print('\\\\nGLOBAL',mode,'variance ratios',np.round(vals[:2]/vals.sum(),4))\\n l=pd.DataFrame(vec[:,:2],index=f,columns=['PC1','PC2'])\\n # orient PC1 to positive aggregate on three requested indicators; PC2 arbitrary but set largest absolute variable positive\\n if l.loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert'],'PC1'].sum()<0:l['PC1']*=-1\\n if l.PC2.iloc[np.argmax(np.abs(l.PC2))]<0:l['PC2']*=-1\\n print(l.loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].round(4).to_string())\\n print('all loadings')\\n print(l.round(3).to_string())\\nprint('\\\\nGroup means symbols')\\nprint(x.groupby('group',sort=False)[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].mean().round(3).to_string())\\nPY\",\"timeout_seconds\":30}"
      }

        "arguments": "{\"summary\":\"Run alternative PCA loading interpretation diagnostics\",\"command\":\"python - <<'PY'\\nimport pandas as pd,numpy as np\\nx=pd.read_csv('data/capital.csv',dtype=str); f=x.columns[3:]\\nfor c in f:x[c]=pd.to_numeric(x[c].str.replace(',','.'))\\na=x[f].values.astype(float); a-=a.mean(0); a/=a.std(0,ddof=1)\\n_,s,vt=np.linalg.svd(a,full_matrices=False)\\nL=vt[:2].T\\nfor j in range(2):\\n if L[np.argmax(abs(L[:,j])),j]<0:L[:,j]*=-1\\nprint(pd.DataFrame(L,index=f,columns=['PC1_loading','PC2_loading']).loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].round(4))\\nprint('scores first/last chronology:')\\nprint(pd.DataFrame(a@L,index=x.BCE.astype(int),columns=['PC1','PC2']).loc[[4300,3500,3000,2300,2200,1700,1600,1200,1100,500]].round(3))\\nPY\",\"timeout_seconds\":20}"
      }

Symbolic capital consists of Hatchet & Swords, Daggers, House Size. 2. Social Capital consists of Copper and Gold, Amber, Monument Count 3. Cultural Capital consists of Diversity of Pottery form, Diversity of Pottery Decoration. 4. Economic Capital consists of Depot, Sickle, Axes & Celts, Monument Size. 5. Human impact or landscape openness, respectively, as reflected in the pollen data, can be used as a demographic indicator based on the assumption that an increasing population density leads to increasing woodland clearance due to an increasing demand for resources including wood, agricultural land and settlement areas. Each sample from the pollen record used in the principal component analysis is absolutely dated and therefore the openness score (PC 1.) can be plotted as a time series, expressing human induced land clearance. The 'Belau_PC1' of pollen data (pollen_openness_score_Belau_Woserin_Feeser_et_al_2019.csv) has been assumed to reflect openness. Original openness score through 'Belau_PC1', 100-year smoothed openness score, and linear interpolation of openness score have been used to signify growth. The original openness score (Belau_PC1), the 100-year smoothed openness score (pollen_inter_100), and the linear interpolation of the openness score (pollen_inter) have been used to signify openness. 6. Demographic growth manifests itself in growth set bringing with it a further opening of the landscape. Opening of landscape corresponds to higher growth rates. During the Early Neolithic, we are dealing with a population growth that goes hand in hand with the opening up of vegetation and the cultivation of the landscape. The growth rate is defined as the percentage change of the 100-year smoothed openness score (pollen_grate_100). 7.
